Deliberative polling and its scaling problem. Deliberative polling instruments a simple hypothesis: opinion formed after ex osure to balanced ar ument difers s stematicall from o inion measured cold (Fishkin 1991; Fishkin et al. 2000). The canonical desi<sub>g</sub>n draws a stratified sam<sub>p</sub>le, su<sub>pp</sub>lies balanced briefin<sub>g</sub> material, runs moderated small-<sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> discussion<sub>,</sub> and re-measures. Results across man<sub>y</sub> de<sub>p</sub>lo<sub>y</sub>ments are consistent in direction — movement is lar<sub>g</sub>est where the initial <sub>p</sub>osition was least informed (Fishkin 2009; Luskin et al. 2002) — and the efect survives when artisan cues are stri ed from the material (Price et al. 2002). Value-based extensions attach audience-relative <sub>pre</sub>f<sub>erences</sub> t<sub>o argumen</sub>t<sub>s an</sub>d th<sub>ere</sub>b<sub>y exp</sub>l<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n ra</sub>ti<sub>ona</sub>l di<sub>sagreemen</sub>t b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween au</sub>di<sub>ences</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>accep</sub>t th<sub>e same</sub> facts (Bench-Ca<sub>p</sub>on and Dunne 2007), which is close in s<sub>p</sub>irit to the <sub>p</sub>er-voter <sub>p</sub>olic<sub>y</sub> vector of Section 4.6. Our <sub>use o</sub>f thi<sub>s appara</sub>t<sub>us</sub> i<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>lib<sub>era</sub>t<sub>e</sub>l<sub>y s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>ow, an</sub>d th<sub>e s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>owness</sub> i<sub>s a</sub> d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>gn comm</sub>it<sub>men</sub>t<sub>.</sub> W<sub>e a</sub>d<sub>op</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> bi<sub>po</sub>l<sub>ar</sub> si<sub>g</sub>nature — two relations of o<sub>pp</sub>osite <sub>p</sub>olarit<sub>y</sub> over a set of items — and decline the semantics: we com<sub>p</sub>ute no extensions and <sub>p</sub>ronounce no ar<sub>g</sub>ument acce<sub>p</sub>table or defeated. The reason is Criterion 4 (semantic abstinence, Section 4.2) — an<sub>y</sub> semantics decidin<sub>g</sub> which ar<sub>g</sub>uments survive is a machine decidin<sub>g</sub> which ar<sub>g</sub>uments a voter <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>ld <sub>s</sub>t<sub>op cons</sub>id<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ng, exac</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e au</sub>th<sub>or</sub>it<sub>y a c</sub>i<sub>v</sub>i<sub>c s</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e mus</sub>t <sub>no</sub>t d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ega</sub>t<sub>e.</sub> Li<sub>n</sub>k<sub>s</sub> h<sub>ere are ev</sub>id<sub>ence a</sub>b<sub>ou</sub>t re<sup>l</sup>evance, not a<sup>d</sup>ju<sup>d</sup>ication o<sup>f</sup> trut<sup>h</sup>. T<sup>h</sup>at commitment a<sup>l</sup>so <sup>d</sup>istinguis<sup>h</sup>es t<sup>h</sup>is wor<sup>k f</sup>rom <sup>d</sup>ia<sup>l</sup>ectica<sup>l</sup>-qua<sup>l</sup>ity measures over exchan<sub>g</sub>es between <sub>p</sub>arties (Rocha et al. 2026), which jud<sub>g</sub>e the ar<sub>g</sub>umentation; Prakken and Sartor on ar<sub>g</sub>ument schemes in law (Prakken 2001) and the <sub>p</sub>rocedural tradition runnin<sub>g</sub> back to Robert’s rules (Robert 1915) are closer to the role links <sub>p</sub>la<sub>y</sub> here, structurin<sub>g</sub> who ma<sub>y</sub> s<sub>p</sub>eak to what rather than who is ri<sub>g</sub>ht.

Computational social choice for participatory processes. A parallel line asks how collective decisions should be com<sub>p</sub>osed once <sub>p</sub>artici<sub>p</sub>ation is mediated b<sub>y</sub> software. Argument mining extracts claims, premises and relations from text (Lippi and Torroni 2016; Stab and Gurev<sub>y</sub>ch 2017); retrieval s<sub>y</sub>stems rank <sub>p</sub>assa<sub>g</sub>es b<sub>y</sub> ar<sub>g</sub>umentative <sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> (Carenini and Moore 2006; Wachsmuth et al. 2018); and ar<sub>g</sub>umentative dialo<sub>g</sub>ue a<sub>g</sub>ents use such structures to conduct <sub>p</sub>ersuasive exchan<sub>g</sub>es (Chala<sub>g</sub>uine and Hunter 2020). Civic platforms with algorithmic assistance. Deployed systems already make these choices. Polis clusters <sub>p</sub>artici<sub>p</sub>ants b<sub>y</sub> a<sub>g</sub>reement <sub>p</sub>attern and surfaces statements that brid<sub>g</sub>e clusters (Small et al. 2021), an a<sub>pp</sub>roach with a <sub>g</sub>enuine claim to re<sub>p</sub>resentativeness whose clusterin<sub>g</sub> ste<sub>p</sub> nevertheless re<sub>q</sub>uires the o<sub>p</sub>erator’s <sub>p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>eline to be trusted; hybrid participatory systems go further and estimate the values behind participants’ textual motivations, disambi<sub>g</sub>uatin<sub>g</sub> them interactivel<sub>y</sub> (Liscio et al. 2025), which is the closest <sub>p</sub>ublished treatment of the authorin<sub>g</sub>-time inference we <sub>p</sub>lace of the servin<sub>g p</sub>ath. Deliberation-su<sub>pp</sub>ort <sub>p</sub>latforms have been surve<sub>y</sub>ed for their ar<sub>g</sub>umentation structures (Brenneis et al. 2021; Mancini 2015), and recent work examines lan<sub>g</sub>ua<sub>g</sub>e models as facilitators (Behrendt et al. 2024; Tessler et al. 2024), with Tessler et al. re<sub>p</sub>ortin<sub>g</sub> that a model-<sub>g</sub>enerated <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>emen</sub>t <sub>can</sub> fi<sub>n</sub>d <sub>more common groun</sub>d th<sub>an a</sub> h<sub>uman me</sub>di<sub>a</sub>t<sub>or.</sub> Th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>resu</sub>lt i<sub>s rea</sub>l <sub>an</sub>d <sub>we</sub> d<sub>o no</sub>t di<sub>spu</sub>t<sub>e</sub> it<sub>.</sub> O<sub>ur</sub> o<sup>b</sup>jection is categorica<sup>l</sup> rat<sup>h</sup>er t<sup>h</sup>an empirica<sup>l</sup>: a me<sup>d</sup>iator w<sup>h</sup>ose reasoning cannot <sup>b</sup>e reconstructe<sup>d</sup> is unsuita<sup>bl</sup>e f<sub>or a</sub> bi<sub>n</sub>di<sub>ng process regar</sub>dl<sub>ess o</sub>f <sub>measure</sub>d <sub>qua</sub>lit<sub>y,</sub> f<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e same reason</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>a</sub> d<sub>emons</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>bl<sub>y accura</sub>t<sub>e</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t unauditable vote count is unsuitable. The re<sub>g</sub>ulator<sub>y</sub> direction is consistent — the Di<sub>g</sub>ital Services Act obli<sub>g</sub>es ver<sub>y</sub> lar<sub>g</sub>e <sub>p</sub>latforms to disclose recommender <sub>p</sub>arameters and ofer a non-<sub>p</sub>rofilin<sub>g</sub> o<sub>p</sub>tion (Euro<sub>p</sub>ean Parliament and Council 2022), and the AI Act im<sub>p</sub>oses trans<sub>p</sub>arenc<sub>y</sub> and human-oversi<sub>g</sub>ht duties on s<sub>y</sub>stems used in democratic <sub>p</sub>rocesses (Euro<sub>p</sub>ean Parliament and Council 2024) — and s<sub>y</sub>co<sub>p</sub>hanc<sub>y</sub> (Sharma et al. 2023) and measurable <sub>p</sub>olitical leanin<sub>g</sub> in <sub>p</sub>retrained models (Fen<sub>g</sub> et al. 2023) are further reasons to kee<sub>p</sub> such models of the servin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>at<sup>h</sup>.

Diversity, coverage and manipulation in recommendation. Ranking by predicted relevance alone yields redundant lists; maximal mar<sub>g</sub>inal relevance trades relevance a<sub>g</sub>ainst novelt<sub>y</sub> (Carbonell and Goldstein 1998), and a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ate diversit<sub>y</sub> has been studied as a s<sub>y</sub>stem-level objective (Adomavicius and Kwon 2012). Multi-stakeholder recommendation observes that <sub>p</sub>latform, <sub>p</sub>rovider and consumer objectives diver<sub>g</sub>e (Burke 2017); recenc<sub>y</sub>-aware work notes the drift of freshness a ainst ualit (Chakrabort et al. 2019); and conce tual modellin of ex lainable recommenders has ma ed what an ex lanation of a recommendation can even be (Caro-Martínez et al. 2021). Agentic simulation as an evaluation method. Generative agents with memory and planning reproduce plausible social behaviour in sandboxed environments (Park et al. 2023); lan<sub>g</sub>ua<sub>g</sub>e models conditioned on demo<sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hic <sub>p</sub>rofiles re<sub>p</sub>roduce surve<sub>y</sub> res<sub>p</sub>onse distributions (Ar<sub>gy</sub>le et al. 2023); multi-a<sub>g</sub>ent orchestration frameworks have matured (Q. Wu et al. 2024); and the method has been a<sub>pp</sub>lied to social-science <sub>q</sub>uestions directl<sub>y</sub> (Bail 2024). We therefore restrict every claim to statements about mechanism under a stated behavioural model — com<sub>p</sub>arisons between rankin<sub>g</sub> arms<sub>,</sub> sensitivities to structural <sub>p</sub>arameters<sub>,</sub> res<sub>p</sub>onses to <sub>a</sub>tt<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>s</sub> <sub>—</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>ma</sub>k<sub>e</sub> <sub>no</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>a</sub>i<sub>m</sub> <sub>a</sub>b<sub>ou</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> <sub>magn</sub>it<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e</sub> <sub>any</sub> <sub>quan</sub>tit<sub>y</sub> <sub>wou</sub>ld t<sub>a</sub>k<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub> <sub>a</sub> h<sub>uman</sub> <sub>e</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ora</sub>t<sub>e.</sub>

Decentralised infrastructure for civic processes. Structured overlays give scalable key-based routing (Maymounkov and Mazières 2002); e<sub>p</sub>idemic <sub>p</sub>rotocols <sub>g</sub>ive robust eventual dissemination (Demers et al. 1988); conflict-free re<sub>p</sub>licated data t<sub>yp</sub>es <sub>g</sub>ive conver<sub>g</sub>ence without coordination (Sha<sub>p</sub>iro et al. 2011); hash trees <sub>g</sub>ive com<sub>p</sub>act inte<sub>g</sub>rit<sub>y p</sub>roofs (Merkle 1988); blockchains <sub>g</sub>ive a<sub>pp</sub>end-onl<sub>y p</sub>ublic led<sub>g</sub>ers (Nakamoto 2008), with trans<sub>p</sub>arenc<sub>y</sub>-lo<sub>g</sub> constructions a<sub>pp</sub>lied to u<sub>p</sub>date inte<sub>g</sub>rit<sub>y</sub> (Nikitin et al. 2017) and cr<sub>yp</sub>to<sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hic scrutin<sub>y</sub> of a<sub>g</sub>reement <sub>p</sub>rotocols (Miller 2020); and decentralised social <sub>p</sub>latforms have ex<sub>p</sub>lored <sub>g</sub>ossi<sub>p</sub>-based dissemination (Boutet et al. 2013). DDP2P occu<sub>p</sub>ies a s<sub>p</sub>ecific <sub>p</sub>osition: it is not a led<sub>g</sub>er and not a DHT, but a <sub>g</sub>ossi<sub>p</sub>-re<sub>p</sub>licated store of self-contained, si<sub>g</sub>ned items desi<sub>g</sub>ned for <sub>p</sub>etition drives and or<sub>g</sub>anisational decision-makin<sub>g</sub> (M. C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi, Alhamed, et al. 2013; M. C. Silaghi, Qin, Alhamed, et al. 2013; M. C. Silaghi and Roussev 2014). Its research programme covers the pieces a civic deployment actually needs — decentralised census construction and verification (Qin, M. C. Silaghi, Hussien, et al. 2014; Qin, M. C. Silaghi, Matsui, et al. 2013b), detection of false identities (Qin, M. C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi, Matsui, et al. 2013a), <sub>p</sub>eer re<sub>p</sub>utation (Qin, M. C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi, Matsui, et al. 2013c), su<sub>p</sub>ernodes for <sub>p</sub>eers behind NAT (Alhamed and M. C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi 2014), <sub>p</sub>rotocol stackin<sub>g</sub> and u<sub>p</sub>date <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>ation (Alhamed, M. C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi, Hussien, Stansifer, et al. 2013; Alhamed, M. C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi, Hussien, and Yan<sub>g</sub> 2013), meta-level recommendation over decentralised data (Alhamed, Zanker, et al. 2016), trust and ke<sub>y</sub> mana<sub>g</sub>ement (M. C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi, Qin, Matsui, et al. 2016), interface studies for non-ex<sub>p</sub>ert users (Al<sub>q</sub>ahtani and M. C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi 2016, 2017; Kattamuri et al. 2005), its lo<sub>g</sub>ical foundations (Roussev and M. C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi 2017), related vehicular de<sub>p</sub>lo<sub>y</sub>ments (O. Dhannoon et al. 2013; O. A. Dhannoon 2013), and the motivatin<sub>g</sub> ar<sub>g</sub>ument for wh<sub>y p</sub>artici<sub>p</sub>ation is worth the en<sub>g</sub>ineerin<sub>g</sub> (M.-C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi et al. 2017). Section 10 builds on that stack rather than <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>osin<sub>g</sub> a new one.

For a voter � casting a ballot at time $t _ { i } ,$ <sub>,</sub> l<sub>e</sub>t $\mathcal { T } _ { t _ { i } }$ <sup>b</sup>e t<sup>h</sup>e set o<sup>f</sup> justi<sup>fi</sup>cations <sub>su</sub>b<sub>m</sub>itt<sub>e</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> b<sub>e</sub>f<sub>ore</sub> $t _ { i } ,$ and let the live vocabulary on side $\sigma \in \{ + , - \}$ b<sub>e</sub> $\begin{array} { r } { \Lambda _ { t _ { i } } ^ { \sigma } = \bigcup _ { j \in \mathcal { T } ^ { \sigma } \cap \mathcal { T } _ { t _ { i } } } \lambda ( j ) } \end{array}$

Definition 3.4 (Slate completeness). Let $S _ { i } ^ { \sigma } \subseteq \mathcal { T } ^ { \sigma } \cap \mathcal { T } _ { t _ { i } }$ <sup>b</sup>e t<sup>h</sup>e s<sup>l</sup>ate o<sup>f</sup> at most � justi<sup>fi</sup>cations serve<sup>d</sup> to voter � on side �. Its completeness is

$$
c _ { i } ^ { \sigma } ~ = ~ \frac { \big | ~ \bigcup _ { j \in S _ { i } ^ { \sigma } } \lambda ( j ) ~ \big | } { \big | ~ \Lambda _ { t _ { i } } ^ { \sigma } ~ \big | } ~ \in ~ [ 0 , 1 ] ,\tag{2}
$$

<sub>w</sub>ith $c _ { i } ^ { \sigma } = 1$ b<sub>y</sub> <sub>conven</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> $\Lambda _ { t _ { i } } ^ { \sigma } = \emptyset$ . The combined completeness of a run over � voters, by averaging, is

Criterion 1 (Determinism). Given the same evidence and the same policy vector, the mechanism returns the same slate. Test: run it twice on a frozen snapshot; the outputs are identical, ties included. Violation looks like:

T<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> 1<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e seven cr</sub>it<sub>er</sub>i<sub>a a a</sub>i<sub>ns</sub>t th<sub>ree mec</sub>h<sub>an</sub>i<sub>sm</sub> f<sub>am</sub>ili<sub>es. –</sub> d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>es</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>ure</sub> b <sub>cons</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>ti<sub>on ra</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an</sub> b i<sub>m</sub> l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> choice: no amount of en<sub>g</sub>ineerin<sub>g</sub> efort within that famil<sub>y</sub> recovers the <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>ert<sub>y</sub>. The <sub>g</sub>reed<sub>y</sub> cover is included because it a<sub>pp</sub>ears throu<sub>g</sub>hout the evaluation and it is im<sub>p</sub>ortant to be ex<sub>p</sub>licit that it is inadmissible<sub>;</sub> see Princi<sub>p</sub>le 1.

t<sub>wo</sub> <sub>par</sub>ti<sub>c</sub>i<sub>pan</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith id<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>con</sub>fi<sub>gura</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> <sub>an</sub>d id<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>l l<sub>oca</sub>l d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a</sub> <sub>see</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>t <sub>s</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es,</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>no</sub> <sub>pu</sub>bli<sub>s</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d reason.

Criterion 2 (Evidence locality). The score of an item depends only on that item and on a bounded, explicitly specified neighbourhood of it. Test: perturb an item outside the declared neighbourhood; the score does not move. Violation looks like: a global fixed point, or any quantity whose value depends on the whole corpus, which makes b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>par</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>l<sub>-rep</sub>li<sub>ca</sub> <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>an</sub>d l<sub>oca</sub>l <sub>reason</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>a</sub>b<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>a</sub> di<sub>spu</sub>t<sub>e</sub> i<sub>mposs</sub>ibl<sub>e.</sub>

Criterion 3 (Author blindness). No term in the score refers to who wrote an item, beyond counting distinct authors where a policy explicitly calls for it. Test: permute author identities across items; the ranking is invariant. Violation looks like: reputation, seniority, or verified-account status entering the ranking — reintroducing the i<sub>n</sub>fl<sub>uence</sub> hi<sub>erarc</sub>h<sub>y</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>a po</sub>ll i<sub>s suppose</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> fl<sub>a</sub>tt<sub>en.</sub>

Criterion 4 (Semantic abstinence). The serving-time mechanism does not read the content of items. Test: replace every item’s text with an opaque identifier; the slate is unchanged. Violation looks like: the selector deciding, at servin<sub>g</sub> time<sub>,</sub> what an ar<sub>g</sub>ument means or whether it is an<sub>y g</sub>ood — which is the s<sub>p</sub>ecific authorit<sub>y</sub> a civic slate must not dele<sub>g</sub>ate<sub>,</sub> and which the <sub>g</sub>reed<sub>y</sub> cover of Section 3.5 o<sub>p</sub>enl<sub>y</sub> exercises<sub>,</sub> hence Princi<sub>p</sub>le 1.

Criterion 5 (Reproducibility). Every served slate is recomputable after the fact from persisted evidence. Test: from the archive, reconstruct any historical slate exactly, including the state of the corpus at that instant. Violation looks like: an audit that can establish what was tallied but not what was shown.

Criterion 6 (Contestability). Every slate decomposes into named contributions, each traceable to a public artefact. Test: for any served item, produce the list of (term, evidence, value) triples summing to its score. Violation looks like: an explanation that is itself a model output.

Criterion 7 (Configurability). The policy parameters are held by the individual participant receiving it, not by the operator, and the participant can change them and observe the efect. Test: two participants with diferent policies, same evidence, obtain diferent and separately correct slates. Violation looks like: a single operator-chosen rankin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>resented as neutral<sub>,</sub> or a <sub>p</sub>ersonalisation the <sub>p</sub>artici<sub>p</sub>ant cannot ins<sub>p</sub>ect or switch of.

## 6<sub>.</sub>1 Pr<sub>opos</sub>iti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>s</sub>

UBI asks “Should one address the AI revolution by introducing basic income?”, with thirty canonical reasons per side spanning fiscal, labour-market, administrative, distributive and political-economy considerations. BRA asks “Should one address the AI revolution by introducing basic resource assurance (minimal healthy housing, food, and emergency healthcare)?”. A third proposition, OPT-OUT, asks “Should primary school pupils be able to study without computers and an internet connection?”, with thirty canonical reasons per side spanning pedagogical, develo<sub>p</sub>mental<sub>,</sub> e<sub>q</sub>uit<sub>y</sub>-of-access and administrative considerations. What the platform provides. DDP2P is a project developing an open-source platform for decentralised deliberative petition drives, built around commitments that turn out to be the ones the charter needs. Items are self-contained and globally identified: every peer record, organisation, constituent, motion, justification, signature, vote<sub>,</sub> witnessin<sub>g</sub> statement<sub>,</sub> news item and translation carries ever<sub>y</sub>thin<sub>g</sub> needed to inter<sub>p</sub>ret it and is named b<sub>y</sub> an identifier derived from a <sub>p</sub>ublic ke<sub>y</sub> with a creation date, or from a di<sub>g</sub>est of its content (M. C. Sila<sub>g</sub>hi, Alhamed, et al. 2013). Identifiers com<sub>p</sub>ose hierarchicall<sub>y</sub> — an or<sub>g</sub>anisation’s derives from its foundin<sub>g p</sub>arameters, a motion<sup>’</sup>s <sup>f</sup>rom t<sup>h</sup>e organisation an<sup>d</sup> its text, a justi<sup>fi</sup>cation<sup>’</sup>s <sup>f</sup>rom t<sup>h</sup>e motion an<sup>d</sup> its own text, a vote<sup>’</sup>s <sup>f</sup>rom t<sup>h</sup>e motion, t<sup>h</sup>e constituent an<sup>d</sup> t<sup>h</sup>e justi<sup>fi</sup>cation cite<sup>d</sup> — so two peers w<sup>h</sup>o <sup>h</sup>ave never communicate<sup>d</sup> agree on t<sup>h</sup>e name of everything and semantically distinct items cannot collide. Synchronisation is push–pull gossip with a horizon (Demers et al. 1988; M. C. Silaghi, Qin, Alhamed, et al. 2013): a request carries the requester’s interests and <sub>a</sub> ti<sub>me</sub> h<sub>or</sub>i<sub>zon an</sub>d th<sub>e answer carr</sub>i<sub>es</sub> it<sub>ems newer</sub> th<sub>an</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t h<sub>or</sub>i<sub>zon, w</sub>ith <sub>no g</sub>l<sub>o</sub>b<sub>a</sub>l i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>ex an</sub>d <sub>no au</sub>th<sub>or</sub>it<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> replica. Evaluating the rule on an incomplete replica. A peer holds what it has synchronised, generally a subset of what exists. T<sup>h</sup>is is t<sup>h</sup>e s<sup>h</sup>arpest tec<sup>h</sup>nica<sup>l</sup> o<sup>b</sup>jection to <sup>l</sup>oca<sup>l</sup> eva<sup>l</sup>uation an<sup>d</sup> Proposition 3.8 on<sup>l</sup>y <sup>h</sup>a<sup>lf</sup> answers it.

Proposition 10.1 (Monotone degradation). Let $\mathcal { T } ^ { \prime } \subseteq \mathcal { T } ^ { \sigma }$ be the items a peer holds and $S ^ { \prime }$ the slate Algorithm 1 produces from them. Then $S ^ { \prime }$ is exactly the slate the same rule and policy would produce on the full pool restricted to ${ \mathcal { T } } ^ { \prime }$ . Missing items can only lower achievable completeness, never corrupt the score of a held item. Missing votes and links, however, lower the computed score ofa held item, so the peer’s scores are lower bounds on the true ones.

## A The Com<sub>p</sub>arison This Manuscri<sub>p</sub>t Declines to Run

Section 4.3 states in the bod<sub>y</sub> wh<sub>y</sub> no learned-ranker baseline a<sub>pp</sub>ears in this manuscri<sub>p</sub>t. This a<sub>pp</sub>endix records <sub>w</sub>h<sub>a</sub>t <sub>suc</sub>h <sub>an</sub> <sub>exper</sub>i<sub>men</sub>t <sub>wou</sub>ld h<sub>ave</sub> t<sub>o</sub> l<sub>oo</sub>k lik<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub> b<sub>e</sub> <sub>wor</sub>th <sub>runn</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub>

The claim is normative and concerns admissibilit<sub>y,</sub> not <sub>p</sub>erformance. It sa<sub>y</sub>s that a mechanism which cannot be recom<sub>p</sub>uted<sub>,</sub> attributed<sub>,</sub> contested or reconstructed is unsuitable for a bindin<sub>g</sub> civic <sub>p</sub>rocess. A com<sub>p</sub>arison a<sub>g</sub>ainst a learned ranker would re<sub>p</sub>ort a diference in covera<sub>g</sub>e<sub>,</sub> in orderin<sub>g,</sub> or in some downstream en<sub>g</sub>a<sub>g</sub>ement statistic. Whatever number emerged could not bear on the claim: a learned ranker covering more of the live vocabular<sub>y</sub> would still fail C2<sub>,</sub> C4<sub>,</sub> C5 and C6<sub>,</sub> and would still leave a dis<sub>p</sub>utin<sub>g p</sub>artici<sub>p</sub>ant with no terminatin<sub>g</sub> move. Runnin<sub>g</sub> the com<sub>p</sub>arison and declinin<sub>g</sub> to act on its result would be theatre<sub>;</sub> runnin<sub>g</sub> it and actin<sub>g</sub> on its result would concede that le<sub>g</sub>ibilit<sub>y</sub> is a <sub>q</sub>uantit<sub>y</sub> to be traded<sub>,</sub> which is the <sub>p</sub>osition this manuscri<sub>p</sub>t exists to reject.
